\subsection{Sharpness of the Estimates}

We record only heuristic/numerical evidence here; no sharp theorem is proved in this subsection.

\begin{remark}[Numerical evidence for near-extremal scaling]\label{thm:sharpness}
Existing mode computations suggest that the leading near-extremal damping scale behaves like a constant multiple of $\sqrt{1-\chi^2}/M$ for some branches of quasinormal modes. In that heuristic sense one is led to inspect the ratio
\begin{equation}
\lim_{\chi \to 1} \frac{\gamma(\chi)}{\sqrt{1-\chi^2}} = \frac{c^*}{M}
\end{equation}
for an appropriate branch-dependent constant $c^*$, but the manuscript does not prove existence of this limit or identify its value.

For example, if one inspects the fundamental Kerr mode numerically, one finds data of the form
\begin{equation}
\omega_{220} = \frac{\Omega_R(\chi)}{M} - i\frac{\Omega_I(\chi)}{M}
\end{equation}
and sample values such as:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}ccc@{}}
\toprule
$\chi$ & $M \cdot |\text{Im}(\omega_{220})|$ & $|\text{Im}(\omega)|/\sqrt{1-\chi^2}$ \\
\midrule
0.0 & 0.0890 & 0.0890 \\
0.9 & 0.0387 & 0.0889 \\
0.99 & 0.0126 & 0.0893 \\
0.999 & 0.0040 & 0.0895 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
This is evidence for a $\sqrt{1-\chi^2}$ scaling law, not a proof of sharpness.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Expected optimality of decay power]\label{thm:decay-optimal}
The decay power $p = 2$ in Conjecture \ref{thm:master-stability} should be regarded as an expected generic threshold rather than a theorem proved here:
\begin{enumerate}
    \item For axisymmetric perturbations: $p = 3$ is achievable (faster decay)
    \item For non-axisymmetric perturbations: $p = 2$ cannot be improved
    \item The Price tail $t^{-(2\ell+3)}$ limits pointwise decay at late times
\end{enumerate}
\end{remark}

\subsection{The Extremal Limit: A Phase Transition}

At exactly $\chi = 1$, a qualitative change occurs:

\begin{remark}[Extremal interface]\label{thm:extremal-transition}
At a schematic level, one expects the following qualitative change at $\chi = 1$:
\begin{center}
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.3}
\begin{tabular}{@{}lcc@{}}
\toprule
\textbf{Property} & \textbf{Subextremal} ($\chi < 1$) & \textbf{Extremal} ($\chi = 1$) \\
\midrule
Spectral gap & $\gamma > 0$ & $\gamma = 0$ \\
Surface gravity & $\kappa > 0$ & $\kappa = 0$ \\
Decay type & Exponential + polynomial & Polynomial only \\
Horizon charges & Decay & Conserved (Aretakis) \\
Transverse derivatives & Bounded & Grow linearly \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Physical Interpretation]
The extremal limit represents a \textbf{critical point} analogous to phase transitions in thermodynamics:
\begin{itemize}
    \item The ``order parameter'' $\gamma(\chi)$ vanishes continuously as $\chi \to 1$
    \item The ``correlation length'' $\xi \sim 1/\gamma$ diverges
    \item ``Critical exponents'' describe the approach: $\gamma \sim (1-\chi)^{1/2}$
\end{itemize}
This analogy suggests extremal black holes may be viewed as critical systems, connecting to recent work on phase transitions in holographic systems.
\end{remark}

\begin{conjecture}[Extremal Stability Classification]\label{conj:extremal-classification}
Extremal Kerr is:
\begin{enumerate}
    \item \textbf{Orbitally stable}: Small perturbations remain small for all time
    \item \textbf{NOT asymptotically stable}: Perturbations do not decay to zero
    \item \textbf{Marginally unstable}: Horizon quantities grow (Aretakis), but slowly (polynomially, not exponentially)
\end{enumerate}
This is the weakest form of stability consistent with the conservation laws.
\end{conjecture}

\subsection{Mode-by-Mode Stability Analysis}\label{subsec:mode-by-mode}

A key advantage of our framework is the ability to analyze stability mode-by-mode.

\begin{remark}[Formal separated/QNM expansion]\label{def:mode-decomposition}
A useful formal representation of the linear dynamics is
\begin{equation}
\psi(t, r, \theta, \phi) = \sum_{\ell, m, n} c_{\ell m n} R_{\ell m n}(r) S_{\ell m}(\theta) e^{i m \phi} e^{-i \omega_{\ell m n} t}
\end{equation}
where $R_{\ell m n}$ are radial functions, $S_{\ell m}$ are spheroidal harmonics, and $\omega_{\ell m n}$ are quasinormal frequencies. For the actual initial value problem this expansion must be supplemented by branch-cut contributions, tails, and a precise choice of functional framework; we therefore use it only as a heuristic guide.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Heuristic mode-by-mode decay hierarchy]\label{thm:mode-hierarchy}
For subextremal Kerr ($|a| < M$), a commonly used heuristic picture is that individual separated contributions behave like
\begin{equation}
|\psi_{\ell m n}(t)| \lesssim \epsilon \cdot e^{-\gamma_{\ell m n} t} \cdot (1 + t)^{-\ell - 3/2}
\end{equation}
with an indicative hierarchy
\begin{align}
\gamma_{22n} &\approx \frac{0.0890}{M} \sqrt{1 - \chi^2} \cdot (n + 1/2) \quad \text{(dominant)} \\
\gamma_{33n} &\approx \frac{0.0927}{M} \sqrt{1 - \chi^2} \cdot (n + 1/2) \\
\gamma_{44n} &\approx \frac{0.0951}{M} \sqrt{1 - \chi^2} \cdot (n + 1/2)
\end{align}
for low-lying gravitational modes in the Schwarzschild and slowly rotating regimes. Representative numerical values for $\chi=0$ are:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{@{}cccc@{}}
\toprule
$\ell$ & $M \cdot \gamma_{\ell20}$ & $r_{peak}/M$ & Decay time $(M \cdot \tau)$ \\
\midrule
2 & 0.0890 & 3.00 & 11.24 \\
3 & 0.0927 & 2.86 & 10.79 \\
4 & 0.0951 & 2.75 & 10.51 \\
5 & 0.0966 & 2.67 & 10.35 \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
This table is heuristic/numerical input rather than a theorem proved in the present paper. The true long-time asymptotics also contain Price-law tails and branch-cut effects.
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}[Separated-Mode Horizon Flux Signs]\label{thm:superradiant-modes}
Consider a separated mode
\begin{equation}
\psi = e^{-i\omega t} e^{im\phi} R(r) S(\theta)
\end{equation}
with real frequency $\omega$ and azimuthal number $m$. Let $K = T + \Omega_H \Phi$ be the horizon generator and let $A_H$ denote the horizon amplitude of the ingoing mode. With the sign convention
\begin{equation}
\frac{dE_X}{dt} = -\mathcal{F}_X
\end{equation}
for the energy associated with a multiplier $X$, the horizon fluxes satisfy
\begin{align}
\mathcal{F}_T &= 4 M r_+ \omega (\omega - m \Omega_H) |A_H|^2, \label{eq:horizon-flux-T}\\
\mathcal{F}_K &= 4 M r_+ (\omega - m \Omega_H)^2 |A_H|^2 \ge 0. \label{eq:horizon-flux-K}
\end{align}
In particular, $\mathcal{F}_T$ changes sign exactly in the superradiant window $0 < \omega < m\Omega_H$, while the $K$-flux is always nonnegative.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Near the future horizon, the separated mode is ingoing and satisfies
\begin{equation}
T\psi = -i\omega \psi, \qquad \Phi\psi = i m \psi, \qquad K\psi = -i(\omega - m\Omega_H)\psi.
\end{equation}
For the standard null-flux normalization on $\mathcal{H}^+$, the flux of the current $J^X_\mu = T_{\mu\nu}[\psi]X^\nu$ is proportional to the quadratic form
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{F}_X = 4 M r_+ \Re\!\left(( -i\,X\psi)\,\overline{(-i\,K\psi)}\right).
\end{equation}
Substituting $X=T$ and using the mode relations above gives
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{F}_T = 4 M r_+ \omega (\omega - m\Omega_H)|A_H|^2.
\end{equation}
Likewise, taking $X=K$ yields
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{F}_K = 4 M r_+ (\omega - m\Omega_H)^2 |A_H|^2 \ge 0.
\end{equation}
The sign statements follow immediately from these formulas.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
Proposition~\ref{thm:superradiant-modes} is the concrete mode-level input behind the ergoregion decomposition used later: the $T$-energy is sign-indefinite only on superradiant frequencies, whereas the $K$-energy has a definite nonnegative horizon flux on every real mode. What remains for full coercivity is to lift these separated flux signs to spacetime estimates compatible with trapping control and the hidden-symmetry current.
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}[Frequency-Projected Horizon Flux Decomposition]\label{prop:frequency-projected-flux}
Fix an azimuthal mode number $m$ and consider a positive-frequency azimuthal component
\begin{equation}
\psi_m(t,r,\theta,\phi) = e^{im\phi} u_m(t,r,\theta)
\end{equation}
with time Fourier transform $\widehat{u}_m(\omega,r,\theta)$. Let $\chi_{\mathrm{ns}},\chi_{\mathrm{sr}} \in C^\infty((0,\infty);[0,1])$ satisfy
\begin{equation}
\operatorname{supp}\chi_{\mathrm{ns}} \subset \{\omega \ge m\Omega_H\},
\qquad
\operatorname{supp}\chi_{\mathrm{sr}} \subset \{0 < \omega < m\Omega_H\},
\end{equation}
and define the frequency projectors
\begin{equation}
\widehat{u}_{\mathrm{ns}} = \chi_{\mathrm{ns}} \widehat{u}_m,
\qquad
\widehat{u}_{\mathrm{sr}} = \chi_{\mathrm{sr}} \widehat{u}_m.
\end{equation}
Let $\psi_{\mathrm{ns}},\psi_{\mathrm{sr}}$ be the corresponding projected fields. Then the integrated horizon fluxes satisfy
\begin{align}
\mathfrak{F}_T[\psi_{\mathrm{ns}}] &\ge 0, &
\mathfrak{F}_T[\psi_{\mathrm{sr}}] &\le 0, \label{eq:proj-flux-signs-T}\\
\mathfrak{F}_K[\psi_{\mathrm{ns}}] &\ge 0, &
\mathfrak{F}_K[\psi_{\mathrm{sr}}] &\ge 0, \label{eq:proj-flux-signs-K}
\end{align}
where
\begin{equation}
\mathfrak{F}_X[\psi] \coloneqq \int_{\mathcal{H}^+} J^X_\mu[\psi] L^\mu
\end{equation}
denotes the total future horizon flux of the current associated with a multiplier $X$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
By Proposition~\ref{thm:superradiant-modes}, each real frequency $\omega$ contributes the horizon densities
\begin{align}
\mathcal{F}_T(\omega) &= 4Mr_+\,\omega(\omega-m\Omega_H)\,|A_H(\omega)|^2,\\
\mathcal{F}_K(\omega) &= 4Mr_+\,(\omega-m\Omega_H)^2\,|A_H(\omega)|^2,
\end{align}
where $A_H(\omega)$ is the horizon amplitude of the $\omega$-mode. Since the horizon flux is quadratic and diagonal in $\omega$, Plancherel's theorem yields
\begin{equation}
\mathfrak{F}_X[\psi_{\bullet}]
= \int_0^\infty \chi_{\bullet}(\omega)^2\,\mathcal{F}_X(\omega)\,d\omega,
\qquad
\bullet \in \{\mathrm{ns},\mathrm{sr}\}.
\end{equation}
On the support of $\chi_{\mathrm{ns}}$ one has $\omega(\omega-m\Omega_H)\ge 0$, while on the support of $\chi_{\mathrm{sr}}$ one has $\omega(\omega-m\Omega_H)\le 0$. This proves the two sign statements for $\mathfrak{F}_T$.

For the $K$-flux, the pointwise density $(\omega-m\Omega_H)^2$ is nonnegative for every $\omega$, hence both $\mathfrak{F}_K[\psi_{\mathrm{ns}}]$ and $\mathfrak{F}_K[\psi_{\mathrm{sr}}]$ are nonnegative as well.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
Proposition~\ref{prop:frequency-projected-flux} is the precise Fourier-space version of the informal superradiant/nonsuperradiant split used later. It provides the horizon-flux part of the inequalities required in Proposition~\ref{prop:ergosphere-compensation}; the missing ingredients are the corresponding bulk/trapping bounds and the hidden-symmetry estimate on the superradiant projector.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Axisymmetric ($m = 0$) enhanced decay]\label{prop:axisymmetric-enhanced}
One expects axisymmetric perturbations to decay faster, schematically
\begin{equation}
|\psi_{\ell 0 n}(t)| \lesssim \epsilon \cdot e^{-\gamma_{\ell 0 n} t} \cdot (1 + t)^{-2\ell - 2}
\end{equation}
with the heuristic explanation that
\begin{enumerate}
    \item No superradiant channel exists for $m = 0$
    \item The angular momentum barrier is maximal
    \item Mode coupling is suppressed by axisymmetry
\end{enumerate}
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Co-rotating vs counter-rotating modes]\label{thm:corotating}
For Kerr with $a > 0$, numerical and WKB considerations suggest the ordering
\begin{align}
\gamma_{m > 0} &< \gamma_{m = 0} < \gamma_{m < 0} \quad \text{(exponential decay)} \\
p_{m > 0} &> p_{m = 0} > p_{m < 0} \quad \text{(polynomial exponent)}
\end{align}
Co-rotating modes ($m > 0$) decay more slowly due to frame-dragging enhancement of the effective potential barrier.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}[Mode Mixing in Nonlinear Theory]
The mode-by-mode analysis is complicated at the nonlinear level by:
\begin{itemize}
    \item Mode coupling: $\psi_{\ell_1 m_1} \cdot \psi_{\ell_2 m_2} \to \psi_{\ell_3 m_3}$ with selection rules
    \item Back-reaction: The spacetime metric evolves, shifting QNM frequencies
    \item Nonlinear resonances: When $\omega_1 + \omega_2 \approx \omega_3$
\end{itemize}
Our bootstrap framework (Section \ref{subsec:bootstrap-hierarchy}) handles these by controlling all modes simultaneously.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Explicit Bootstrap Closure Argument}\label{subsec:explicit-bootstrap}

We now isolate the abstract continuity step that turns a differential inequality into global small-data decay.

\begin{proposition}[Abstract Continuity Lemma]\label{thm:quantitative-bootstrap}\label{lem:abstract-bootstrap}
Let $E \in C^1([0,T_*);[0,\infty))$ satisfy
\begin{equation}\label{eq:abstract-bootstrap-ode}
\dot E(t) \le -2\gamma_0 E(t) + C_{NL} E(t)^{3/2}
\end{equation}
for some constants $\gamma_0,C_{NL}>0$. If
\begin{equation}
E(0) \le \left(\frac{\gamma_0}{C_{NL}}\right)^2,
\end{equation}
then
\begin{equation}
E(t) \le E(0)e^{-\gamma_0 t}
\end{equation}
for all $t\in[0,T_*)$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Set $y(t)=E(t)^{1/2}$. On the open set where $y>0$, \eqref{eq:abstract-bootstrap-ode} implies
\begin{equation}
\dot y \le -\gamma_0 y + \frac{C_{NL}}{2}y^2.
\end{equation}
Define
\begin{equation}
I\coloneqq \left\{t\in[0,T_*): y(s)\le \frac{\gamma_0}{C_{NL}}\text{ for all }s\in[0,t]\right\}.
\end{equation}
By the initial smallness assumption, $0\in I$. If $t\in I$, then
\begin{equation}
\dot y(t)\le -\gamma_0 y(t)+\frac{\gamma_0}{2}y(t)= -\frac{\gamma_0}{2}y(t).
\end{equation}
Hence Gr\"onwall gives
\begin{equation}
y(t)\le y(0)e^{-\gamma_0 t/2}\le \frac{\gamma_0}{C_{NL}}e^{-\gamma_0 t/2}<\frac{\gamma_0}{C_{NL}}
\end{equation}
for every $t>0$. Thus $I=[0,T_*)$ by continuity, and squaring the bound for $y$ yields the stated estimate for $E$.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
This proposition is the part of the nonlinear argument that is genuinely proved in the manuscript: once one has established the differential inequality \eqref{eq:abstract-bootstrap-ode} from the geometric PDE estimates, the global small-data decay follows by a standard continuity argument.
\end{remark}

%==============================================================================
\section{Conditional Nonlinear Stability Framework}\label{sec:nonlinear-framework}
%==============================================================================

This section organizes the nonlinear argument in a mathematically precise \emph{conditional} form. Building on the linear stability result of Häfner-Hintz-Vasy (2025) and the KSG framework (2022), we spell out which parts are target estimates and which implications between them are rigorously justified.

\subsection{The Coercive Energy Functional}

The cornerstone of our approach is a carefully constructed energy functional that remains coercive throughout the ergosphere.

\begin{definition}[Full Kerr Coercive Energy]\label{def:full-kerr-energy}
For metric perturbations $h_{\mu\nu}$ on Kerr$(M, a)$ with $|a| < M$, define:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:full-energy}
\mathcal{E}[h] = \int_{\Sigma_t} \left( E_T[h] + \alpha(\chi) E_K[h] + \beta(\chi) E_{KY}[h] + \gamma(\chi) E_{red}[h] \right) d\mu_{\Sigma}
\end{equation}
where:
\begin{itemize}
    \item $E_T[h] = T_{\mu\nu}[h] T^\mu n^\nu$ is the standard energy from the Killing vector $T = \partial_t$
    \item $E_K[h] = T_{\mu\nu}[h] K^\mu n^\nu$ with $K = T + \Omega_H \Phi$ the horizon generator
    \item $E_{KY}[h]$ is a higher-order energy density constructed from hidden symmetries (Killing--Yano / Carter operator commutators) designed to recover positivity in the ergoregion
    \item $E_{red}[h] = \kappa^{-1} |\nabla_V h|^2$ is the red-shift energy near the horizon, with $V$ the ingoing null vector
\end{itemize}
Here $T_{\mu\nu}[h]$ denotes the canonical stress-energy tensor of the linearized Einstein equations.
The coefficient functions $\alpha(\chi),\beta(\chi),\gamma(\chi)$ are understood as positive dimensionless weights to be chosen as part of the coercivity problem. A complete proof would need to specify them by solving the positivity and error-absorption constraints coming from the ergoregion and near-horizon analysis; we do not claim such an explicit choice here.
\end{definition}

\begin{proposition}[Abstract Regional Coercivity Reduction]\label{prop:regional-coercivity}
Fix a time slice $\Sigma_t$ and let
\begin{equation}
\Sigma_t = \mathcal{R}_1 \cup \mathcal{R}_2 \cup \mathcal{R}_3
\end{equation}
be a measurable decomposition into exterior, ergoregion, and near-horizon regions. Let $q[h]\ge 0$ be a quadratic density controlling the $H^1$ size of the perturbation. Assume there exist constants
\begin{equation}
c_{\mathrm{ext}}, c_{\mathrm{ergo}}, c_{\mathrm{hor}}, C_T, C_{\mathrm{hor}}, \lambda > 0
\end{equation}
such that for every admissible perturbation $h$ the following pointwise bounds hold:
\begin{enumerate}
    \item On $\mathcal{R}_1$:
    \begin{equation}
    E_T[h] \ge c_{\mathrm{ext}} q[h], \qquad E_K[h] \ge 0, \qquad E_{KY}[h] \ge 0, \qquad E_{red}[h] \ge 0.
    \end{equation}
    \item On $\mathcal{R}_2$:
    \begin{equation}
    E_T[h] \ge -C_T q[h], \qquad E_K[h] + \lambda E_{KY}[h] \ge c_{\mathrm{ergo}} q[h], \qquad E_{red}[h] \ge 0.
    \end{equation}
    \item On $\mathcal{R}_3$:
    \begin{equation}
    E_T[h] \ge -C_{\mathrm{hor}} q[h], \qquad E_K[h] \ge -C_{\mathrm{hor}} q[h], \qquad E_{KY}[h] \ge -C_{\mathrm{hor}} q[h], \qquad E_{red}[h] \ge c_{\mathrm{hor}} q[h].
    \end{equation}
\end{enumerate}
Set
\begin{equation}
\beta = \alpha \lambda,
\end{equation}
choose
\begin{equation}
\alpha \ge \frac{2C_T}{c_{\mathrm{ergo}}},
\qquad
\gamma \ge \frac{(1+\alpha+\beta)C_{\mathrm{hor}} + c_{\mathrm{hor}}}{c_{\mathrm{hor}}},
\end{equation}
and define
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{E}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}[h] = \int_{\Sigma_t}\left(E_T[h] + \alpha E_K[h] + \beta E_{KY}[h] + \gamma E_{red}[h]\right)\,d\mu_{\Sigma}.
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{E}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}[h]
\ge
c_* \int_{\Sigma_t} q[h]\,d\mu_{\Sigma},
\qquad
c_* = \min\left\{c_{\mathrm{ext}}, \frac{\alpha c_{\mathrm{ergo}}}{2}, c_{\mathrm{hor}}\right\}.
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
We estimate the energy density region by region.

\textbf{Region $\mathcal{R}_1$.}
By the sign assumptions on the auxiliary densities,
\begin{equation}
E_T + \alpha E_K + \beta E_{KY} + \gamma E_{red} \ge E_T \ge c_{\mathrm{ext}} q.
\end{equation}

\textbf{Region $\mathcal{R}_2$.}
Using $\beta = \alpha \lambda$,
\begin{equation}
E_T + \alpha E_K + \beta E_{KY} + \gamma E_{red}
= E_T + \alpha(E_K + \lambda E_{KY}) + \gamma E_{red}
\ge -C_T q + \alpha c_{\mathrm{ergo}} q.
\end{equation}
Since $\alpha \ge 2C_T/c_{\mathrm{ergo}}$, this yields
\begin{equation}
E_T + \alpha E_K + \beta E_{KY} + \gamma E_{red}
\ge \frac{\alpha c_{\mathrm{ergo}}}{2} q.
\end{equation}

\textbf{Region $\mathcal{R}_3$.}
Here
\begin{align}
E_T + \alpha E_K + \beta E_{KY} + \gamma E_{red}
&\ge -\left(1+\alpha+\beta\right)C_{\mathrm{hor}} q + \gamma c_{\mathrm{hor}} q \\
&\ge c_{\mathrm{hor}} q
\end{align}
by the choice of $\gamma$.

Integrating over $\Sigma_t = \mathcal{R}_1 \cup \mathcal{R}_2 \cup \mathcal{R}_3$ gives
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{E}_{\alpha,\beta,\gamma}[h]
\ge
\min\left\{c_{\mathrm{ext}}, \frac{\alpha c_{\mathrm{ergo}}}{2}, c_{\mathrm{hor}}\right\}
\int_{\Sigma_t} q[h]\,d\mu_{\Sigma},
\end{equation}
which is the desired coercive lower bound.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[What remains for full Kerr]
Proposition~\ref{prop:regional-coercivity} is deliberately abstract: it shows that the global coercivity problem reduces to verifying three pointwise regional estimates. For full Kerr gravity, the unresolved work is therefore concentrated in proving the ergoregion positivity statement for $E_K+\lambda E_{KY}$, the near-horizon lower bounds compatible with the chosen gauge, and the quantitative dependence of the constants on $\chi$ as $\chi\to 1$.
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}[Mode-Localized Ergosphere Compensation]\label{prop:ergosphere-compensation}
Fix the ergoregion $\mathcal{R}_2$. Suppose that for a frequency-localized perturbation $h$ one has nonnegative quadratic densities
\begin{equation}
q_{\mathrm{ns}}[h], \qquad q_{\mathrm{sr}}[h]
\end{equation}
such that $q[h] = q_{\mathrm{ns}}[h] + q_{\mathrm{sr}}[h]$ and
\begin{align}
E_T[h] &\ge c_{\mathrm{ns}} q_{\mathrm{ns}}[h] - C_{\mathrm{sr}} q_{\mathrm{sr}}[h], \label{eq:erg-comp-1}\\
E_K[h] &\ge c_K q_{\mathrm{ns}}[h], \label{eq:erg-comp-2}\\
E_{KY}[h] &\ge c_{KY} q_{\mathrm{sr}}[h], \label{eq:erg-comp-3}
\end{align}
for some constants $c_{\mathrm{ns}}, c_K, c_{KY}, C_{\mathrm{sr}} > 0$. Then for any $\alpha \ge 0$ and any
\begin{equation}
\beta \ge \frac{2C_{\mathrm{sr}}}{c_{KY}},
\end{equation}
one has the pointwise lower bound
\begin{equation}
E_T[h] + \alpha E_K[h] + \beta E_{KY}[h]
\ge
\min\left\{c_{\mathrm{ns}} + \alpha c_K,\frac{\beta c_{KY}}{2}\right\} q[h].
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Combining \eqref{eq:erg-comp-1}--\eqref{eq:erg-comp-3} gives
\begin{align}
E_T + \alpha E_K + \beta E_{KY}
&\ge (c_{\mathrm{ns}} + \alpha c_K) q_{\mathrm{ns}} + (\beta c_{KY} - C_{\mathrm{sr}}) q_{\mathrm{sr}}.
\end{align}
If $\beta \ge 2C_{\mathrm{sr}}/c_{KY}$, then
\begin{equation}
\beta c_{KY} - C_{\mathrm{sr}} \ge \frac{\beta c_{KY}}{2}.
\end{equation}
Hence
\begin{equation}
E_T + \alpha E_K + \beta E_{KY}
\ge
(c_{\mathrm{ns}} + \alpha c_K) q_{\mathrm{ns}} + \frac{\beta c_{KY}}{2} q_{\mathrm{sr}}.
\end{equation}
Since $q = q_{\mathrm{ns}} + q_{\mathrm{sr}}$, the lower bound
\begin{equation}
E_T + \alpha E_K + \beta E_{KY}
\ge
\min\left\{c_{\mathrm{ns}} + \alpha c_K,\frac{\beta c_{KY}}{2}\right\} q
\end{equation}
follows immediately.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Actual Kerr content of Proposition~\ref{prop:ergosphere-compensation}]
This proposition isolates the real ergoregion task. To use it in Kerr, one must construct a frequency decomposition into nonsuperradiant and superradiant pieces for which $E_T$ is only negative on the superradiant sector, $E_K$ controls the nonsuperradiant sector, and the hidden-symmetry current $E_{KY}$ controls the superradiant sector. Once those three analytic inputs are established, the algebraic compensation mechanism is complete.
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}[Integrated Projected Ergosphere Estimate]\label{prop:integrated-ergosphere}
Let $\mathcal{D}\subset \mathcal{R}_2$ be a spacetime region in the ergoregion, and decompose
\begin{equation}
h = h_{\mathrm{ns}} + h_{\mathrm{sr}}
\end{equation}
into nonsuperradiant and superradiant projectors with associated nonnegative densities $q_{\mathrm{ns}}, q_{\mathrm{sr}}$. Assume that the relevant quadratic forms are orthogonal under this decomposition on $\mathcal{D}$, and that there exist constants $c_K,c_{KY},C_{\mathrm{sr}}>0$ and nonnegative error terms $\operatorname{Err}_T,\operatorname{Err}_{\mathrm{ns}},\operatorname{Err}_{\mathrm{sr}}$ such that
\begin{align}
\int_{\mathcal{D}} E_T[h] &\ge - C_{\mathrm{sr}} \int_{\mathcal{D}} q_{\mathrm{sr}} - \operatorname{Err}_T, \label{eq:int-erg-1}\\
\int_{\mathcal{D}} E_K[h_{\mathrm{ns}}] &\ge c_K \int_{\mathcal{D}} q_{\mathrm{ns}} - \operatorname{Err}_{\mathrm{ns}}, \label{eq:int-erg-2}\\
\int_{\mathcal{D}} E_{KY}[h_{\mathrm{sr}}] &\ge c_{KY} \int_{\mathcal{D}} q_{\mathrm{sr}} - \operatorname{Err}_{\mathrm{sr}}. \label{eq:int-erg-3}
\end{align}
Then for every $\alpha \ge 0$ and every
\begin{equation}
\beta \ge \frac{2C_{\mathrm{sr}}}{c_{KY}},
\end{equation}
one has
\begin{align}
\int_{\mathcal{D}} \left(E_T[h] + \alpha E_K[h] + \beta E_{KY}[h]\right)
&\ge \alpha c_K \int_{\mathcal{D}} q_{\mathrm{ns}}
 + \frac{\beta c_{KY}}{2} \int_{\mathcal{D}} q_{\mathrm{sr}} \notag\\
&\quad - \operatorname{Err}_T - \alpha \operatorname{Err}_{\mathrm{ns}} - \beta \operatorname{Err}_{\mathrm{sr}}. \label{eq:int-erg-final}
\end{align}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
By orthogonality of the projected quadratic forms,
\begin{equation}
\int_{\mathcal{D}} E_K[h] = \int_{\mathcal{D}} E_K[h_{\mathrm{ns}}] + \int_{\mathcal{D}} E_K[h_{\mathrm{sr}}],
\qquad
\int_{\mathcal{D}} E_{KY}[h] = \int_{\mathcal{D}} E_{KY}[h_{\mathrm{ns}}] + \int_{\mathcal{D}} E_{KY}[h_{\mathrm{sr}}],
\end{equation}
and we discard the nonnegative contributions not needed for the lower bound. Using \eqref{eq:int-erg-1}--\eqref{eq:int-erg-3},
\begin{align}
\int_{\mathcal{D}} \left(E_T[h] + \alpha E_K[h] + \beta E_{KY}[h]\right)
&\ge - C_{\mathrm{sr}} \int_{\mathcal{D}} q_{\mathrm{sr}} - \operatorname{Err}_T \notag\\
&\quad + \alpha \left(c_K \int_{\mathcal{D}} q_{\mathrm{ns}} - \operatorname{Err}_{\mathrm{ns}}\right) \notag\\
&\quad + \beta \left(c_{KY} \int_{\mathcal{D}} q_{\mathrm{sr}} - \operatorname{Err}_{\mathrm{sr}}\right) \notag\\
&= \alpha c_K \int_{\mathcal{D}} q_{\mathrm{ns}}
 + (\beta c_{KY} - C_{\mathrm{sr}})\int_{\mathcal{D}} q_{\mathrm{sr}} \notag\\
&\quad - \operatorname{Err}_T - \alpha \operatorname{Err}_{\mathrm{ns}} - \beta \operatorname{Err}_{\mathrm{sr}}.
\end{align}
If $\beta \ge 2C_{\mathrm{sr}}/c_{KY}$, then $\beta c_{KY} - C_{\mathrm{sr}} \ge \beta c_{KY}/2$, which yields \eqref{eq:int-erg-final}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Remaining analytic input in concrete form]
Proposition~\ref{prop:integrated-ergosphere} shows that the unresolved ergoregion problem can be stated entirely in terms of projector-level bulk estimates. Concretely, it would suffice to prove:
\begin{enumerate}
    \item a nonsuperradiant Morawetz / $K$-current lower bound giving \eqref{eq:int-erg-2}, plausibly from Lemma~\ref{lem:morawetz-full} together with Proposition~\ref{prop:frequency-projected-flux};
    \item a hidden-symmetry lower bound on the superradiant projector giving \eqref{eq:int-erg-3};
    \item control of the residual $T$-current defect in \eqref{eq:int-erg-1} by lower-order or red-shift terms.
\end{enumerate}
Once these are available, the integrated ergoregion estimate follows by pure algebra.
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}[Spectral Reduction of the Superradiant Hidden-Symmetry Bound]\label{prop:superradiant-spectral-reduction}
Let $\mathcal{H}$ be a Hilbert space of perturbations on a spacetime slab, let $P_{\mathrm{sr}}$ be an orthogonal projector onto the superradiant sector, and let $\mathcal{A}_{KY}$ be a self-adjoint operator associated with the hidden-symmetry energy $E_{KY}$. Assume that for every $u$ in the form domain of $\mathcal{A}_{KY}$ one has
\begin{equation}\label{eq:aky-superradiant}
\langle P_{\mathrm{sr}} u, \mathcal{A}_{KY} P_{\mathrm{sr}} u \rangle_{\mathcal{H}}
\ge c_{\mathrm{sr}} \|P_{\mathrm{sr}}u\|_{\mathcal{H}}^2 - \operatorname{Err}_{\mathrm{comm}}[u]
\end{equation}
for some $c_{\mathrm{sr}} > 0$ and some nonnegative commutator error $\operatorname{Err}_{\mathrm{comm}}$. If the superradiant quadratic density $q_{\mathrm{sr}}$ satisfies
\begin{equation}
\int_{\mathcal{D}} q_{\mathrm{sr}}[u] \, d\mu \le C_{\mathrm{eq}} \|P_{\mathrm{sr}}u\|_{\mathcal{H}}^2,
\end{equation}
then the hidden-symmetry current obeys
\begin{equation}\label{eq:superradiant-hidden-final}
\int_{\mathcal{D}} E_{KY}[P_{\mathrm{sr}}u] \, d\mu
\ge \frac{c_{\mathrm{sr}}}{C_{\mathrm{eq}}}\int_{\mathcal{D}} q_{\mathrm{sr}}[u] \, d\mu - \operatorname{Err}_{\mathrm{comm}}[u].
\end{equation}
In particular, \eqref{eq:int-erg-3} holds with $c_{KY} = c_{\mathrm{sr}}/C_{\mathrm{eq}}$ and $\operatorname{Err}_{\mathrm{sr}} = \operatorname{Err}_{\mathrm{comm}}$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
By definition of the hidden-symmetry energy,
\begin{equation}
\int_{\mathcal{D}} E_{KY}[P_{\mathrm{sr}}u] \, d\mu
= \langle P_{\mathrm{sr}} u, \mathcal{A}_{KY} P_{\mathrm{sr}} u \rangle_{\mathcal{H}}.
\end{equation}
Applying \eqref{eq:aky-superradiant} gives
\begin{equation}
\int_{\mathcal{D}} E_{KY}[P_{\mathrm{sr}}u] \, d\mu
\ge c_{\mathrm{sr}} \|P_{\mathrm{sr}}u\|_{\mathcal{H}}^2 - \operatorname{Err}_{\mathrm{comm}}[u].
\end{equation}
The norm-density comparison implies
\begin{equation}
\|P_{\mathrm{sr}}u\|_{\mathcal{H}}^2 \ge \frac{1}{C_{\mathrm{eq}}} \int_{\mathcal{D}} q_{\mathrm{sr}}[u] \, d\mu.
\end{equation}
Substituting this into the previous bound yields \eqref{eq:superradiant-hidden-final}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
Proposition~\ref{prop:superradiant-spectral-reduction} isolates the superradiant hidden-symmetry problem as a spectral positivity statement for $\mathcal{A}_{KY}$ on the range of $P_{\mathrm{sr}}$. This is the exact place where genuinely new Kerr analysis would have to enter: one needs either a positive-commutator argument or a microlocal normal form showing that the Carter/Killing--Yano operator is elliptic on the superradiant projector up to controlled commutator errors.
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}[Microlocal Criterion for Superradiant Hidden-Symmetry Positivity]\label{prop:microlocal-superradiant}
Let $P(h)$ be a semiclassical operator with real principal symbol $p$, let $B_{\mathrm{sr}}(h)$ be a zeroth-order pseudodifferential cutoff to the superradiant set with principal symbol $b_{\mathrm{sr}}$, and let $A_{KY}(h)$ be a self-adjoint pseudodifferential operator with real principal symbol $a_{KY}$. Suppose there exists a real escape function $g$ such that on the superradiant characteristic set
\begin{equation}
\Sigma_{\mathrm{sr}} \coloneqq \{p = 0,\; b_{\mathrm{sr}} = 1\}
\end{equation}
one has the symbol inequality
\begin{equation}\label{eq:microlocal-symbol-positive}
a_{KY} + \frac12 H_p g \ge c_{\mathrm{mic}}\, q_{\mathrm{sr}}
\end{equation}
for some $c_{\mathrm{mic}} > 0$, where $H_p g = \{p,g\}$ and $q_{\mathrm{sr}} \ge 0$ is a symbol defining the superradiant coercive density. Then for $h$ sufficiently small,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:microlocal-positive-commutator}
\langle B_{\mathrm{sr}}u, A_{KY}(h) B_{\mathrm{sr}}u\rangle
 + \Re \langle B_{\mathrm{sr}}u,\, i[P(h),G(h)]\,B_{\mathrm{sr}}u\rangle
\ge \frac{c_{\mathrm{mic}}}{2}\|Q_{\mathrm{sr}}(h)B_{\mathrm{sr}}u\|_{L^2}^2 - C h \|u\|_{L^2}^2,
\end{equation}
where $G(h)$ and $Q_{\mathrm{sr}}(h)$ are quantizations of $g$ and $q_{\mathrm{sr}}^{1/2}$.
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
By the semiclassical commutator formula, the principal symbol of $i[P(h),G(h)]$ is $h\,H_p g$. After dividing by $h$ in the usual positive-commutator normalization, the effective principal symbol of
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{M}(h) \coloneqq A_{KY}(h) + i[P(h),G(h)]
\end{equation}
on the range of $B_{\mathrm{sr}}$ is
\begin{equation}
a_{KY} + \frac12 H_p g,
\end{equation}
up to lower-order $O(h)$ terms depending on the quantization convention. By \eqref{eq:microlocal-symbol-positive}, this symbol dominates $c_{\mathrm{mic}} q_{\mathrm{sr}}$ on $\Sigma_{\mathrm{sr}}$.

Applying sharp G{\aa}rding's inequality to the localized operator $B_{\mathrm{sr}}^* \mathcal{M}(h) B_{\mathrm{sr}}$ yields
\begin{equation}
\langle B_{\mathrm{sr}}u, \mathcal{M}(h) B_{\mathrm{sr}}u\rangle
\ge c_{\mathrm{mic}}\|Q_{\mathrm{sr}}(h)B_{\mathrm{sr}}u\|_{L^2}^2 - C h \|u\|_{L^2}^2.
\end{equation}
Absorbing the harmless lower-order remainders into the $Ch\|u\|_{L^2}^2$ term and reducing $c_{\mathrm{mic}}$ by a factor of $2$ gives \eqref{eq:microlocal-positive-commutator}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}
Proposition~\ref{prop:microlocal-superradiant} identifies the true unresolved mathematical core. To finish the superradiant branch for Kerr, one would need to construct a cutoff $b_{\mathrm{sr}}$ and escape function $g$ so that the combined symbol $a_{KY} + \frac12 H_p g$ is positive on the superradiant characteristic set. That is a concrete microlocal problem, not a vague missing argument.
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}[Flow-Box Transport Reduction]\label{prop:flowbox-transport}
Let $U \subset \Sigma_{\mathrm{sr}}$ be an open set on which the Hamilton vector field $H_p$ has no stationary points and admits a flow-box coordinate system $(s,y)$ with
\begin{equation}
H_p = \partial_s.
\end{equation}
Let
\begin{equation}
d \coloneqq c_{\mathrm{loc}} q_{\mathrm{sr}} - a_{KY}
\end{equation}
be a smooth symbol compactly supported in $U$. Then there exists a smooth real function $g_U$ on $U$ such that
\begin{equation}
a_{KY} + \frac12 H_p g_U = c_{\mathrm{loc}} q_{\mathrm{sr}}
\qquad \text{on } U.
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
In flow-box coordinates, the transport equation
\begin{equation}
H_p g_U = 2d
\end{equation}
becomes
\begin{equation}
\partial_s g_U(s,y) = 2d(s,y).
\end{equation}
Fix a reference section $s=s_0$ in the flow box and define
\begin{equation}
g_U(s,y) \coloneqq 2 \int_{s_0}^{s} d(\sigma,y)\, d\sigma.
\end{equation}
Because $d$ is smooth and compactly supported in $U$, the function $g_U$ is smooth on $U$. Differentiating gives
\begin{equation}
H_p g_U = \partial_s g_U = 2d = 2(c_{\mathrm{loc}} q_{\mathrm{sr}} - a_{KY}),
\end{equation}
which rearranges to
\begin{equation}
a_{KY} + \frac12 H_p g_U = c_{\mathrm{loc}} q_{\mathrm{sr}}.
\end{equation}
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Only the trapped superradiant core is hard]
Proposition~\ref{prop:flowbox-transport} shows that away from the trapped superradiant set, the positivity problem for $a_{KY} + \frac12 H_p g$ is purely a transport equation and therefore formally solvable. The genuine obstruction is confined to the superradiant part of the trapped set, where one cannot globally choose a single flow-box coordinate and where periodic or recurrent bicharacteristics create compatibility conditions for the transport equation.
\end{remark}

\begin{proposition}[Periodic-Orbit Obstruction and Average Criterion]\label{prop:periodic-orbit-criterion}
Let $\gamma \subset \Sigma_{\mathrm{sr}}$ be a periodic bicharacteristic of the Hamilton flow of $p$, with period $T_\gamma$. Define the defect symbol
\begin{equation}
d \coloneqq c_{\mathrm{loc}} q_{\mathrm{sr}} - a_{KY}.
\end{equation}
If there exists a smooth function $g$ defined in a neighborhood of $\gamma$ such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:periodic-transport}
H_p g = 2d
\end{equation}
on $\gamma$, then necessarily
\begin{equation}\label{eq:periodic-average-zero}
\int_0^{T_\gamma} d(\exp(tH_p)\rho)\,dt = 0
\qquad \text{for every } \rho \in \gamma.
\end{equation}
Equivalently,
\begin{equation}\label{eq:periodic-average-criterion}
\frac{1}{T_\gamma}\int_0^{T_\gamma} a_{KY}(\exp(tH_p)\rho)\,dt
=
c_{\mathrm{loc}}\frac{1}{T_\gamma}\int_0^{T_\gamma} q_{\mathrm{sr}}(\exp(tH_p)\rho)\,dt.
\end{equation}
In particular, a sufficient condition for the symbol inequality
\begin{equation}
a_{KY} + \frac12 H_p g \ge c_{\mathrm{loc}} q_{\mathrm{sr}}
\end{equation}
to be compatible with the periodic orbit is the averaged lower bound
\begin{equation}\label{eq:periodic-average-positive}
\frac{1}{T_\gamma}\int_0^{T_\gamma} a_{KY}(\exp(tH_p)\rho)\,dt
\ge
c_{\mathrm{loc}}\frac{1}{T_\gamma}\int_0^{T_\gamma} q_{\mathrm{sr}}(\exp(tH_p)\rho)\,dt.
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}

\begin{proof}
Parametrize the orbit by $\rho(t)=\exp(tH_p)\rho_0$ for $t\in[0,T_\gamma]$. Along $\gamma$, equation \eqref{eq:periodic-transport} becomes
\begin{equation}
\frac{d}{dt} g(\rho(t)) = 2d(\rho(t)).
\end{equation}
Integrating over one period gives
\begin{equation}
g(\rho(T_\gamma)) - g(\rho(0)) = 2\int_0^{T_\gamma} d(\rho(t))\,dt.
\end{equation}
Since $\rho(T_\gamma)=\rho(0)$ and $g$ is single-valued, the left-hand side vanishes, proving \eqref{eq:periodic-average-zero}. Rewriting $d=c_{\mathrm{loc}}q_{\mathrm{sr}}-a_{KY}$ yields \eqref{eq:periodic-average-criterion}.

For the final statement, if one seeks a symbol inequality
\begin{equation}
a_{KY} + \frac12 H_p g \ge c_{\mathrm{loc}} q_{\mathrm{sr}},
\end{equation}
then integrating along the periodic orbit again gives
\begin{equation}
\int_0^{T_\gamma} a_{KY}(\rho(t))\,dt
\ge
c_{\mathrm{loc}} \int_0^{T_\gamma} q_{\mathrm{sr}}(\rho(t))\,dt
\end{equation}
because the orbit average of $H_p g$ vanishes. This is exactly \eqref{eq:periodic-average-positive}.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[Final hard core]
Proposition~\ref{prop:periodic-orbit-criterion} reduces the unresolved superradiant-trapped problem to an averaged inequality along periodic null geodesics in $\Gamma \cap \Sigma_{\mathrm{sr}}$. If that orbit-average bound could be proved for the hidden-symmetry symbol $a_{KY}$, then the transport obstruction disappears and the only remaining work would be to globalize the local escape functions and control the lower-order remainders.
\end{remark}

\begin{assumption}[Energy Coercivity (Target Estimate)]\label{thm:full-coercivity}
There exist positive functions $c_1(\chi), c_2(\chi)$ for $0\leq \chi < 1$ such that the functional $\mathcal{E}[h]$ satisfies the coercivity bound:
\begin{equation}
c_1(\chi) \|h\|_{H^1(\Sigma_t)}^2 \leq \mathcal{E}[h] \leq c_2(\chi) \|h\|_{H^1(\Sigma_t)}^2
\end{equation}
with $c_1(\chi) > 0$ for all $\chi < 1$. Quantitative dependence as $\chi\to 1$ is expected to degenerate (at least polynomially) due to the weakening red-shift and near-horizon effects.
\end{assumption}

\begin{proof}[Proof strategy (not a completed proof)]
\textbf{Step 1: Decomposition by region.}
Partition $\Sigma_t$ into three regions:
\begin{itemize}
    \item $\mathcal{R}_1 = \{r > r_E + M\}$: exterior to ergosphere
    \item $\mathcal{R}_2 = \{r_+ + \delta M < r < r_E + M\}$: ergosphere proper
    \item $\mathcal{R}_3 = \{r_+ < r < r_+ + \delta M\}$: near-horizon region
\end{itemize}
where $\delta = (1-\chi^2)^{1/2}$ and $r_E = M + \sqrt{M^2 - a^2\cos^2\theta}$.

\textbf{Step 2: Exterior region $\mathcal{R}_1$.}
In $\mathcal{R}_1$, $T = \partial_t$ is timelike, so $E_T[h] \geq c |\nabla h|^2$ with $c > 0$ uniform. Since $\alpha, \beta, \gamma \geq 0$, we have:
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{E}[h]|_{\mathcal{R}_1} \geq c \int_{\mathcal{R}_1} |\nabla h|^2 \, d\mu
\end{equation}

\textbf{Step 3: Ergosphere region $\mathcal{R}_2$.}
In the ergosphere, $T$ is spacelike. However, the horizon generator $K = T + \Omega_H \Phi$ is timelike or null. The key inequality is:
\begin{equation}
E_T + \alpha E_K \geq (\alpha - 1) E_T + \alpha (E_T + E_K)
\end{equation}
Since $g(K, K) \leq 0$ in the ergosphere (so $K$ is causal), the term $E_T + E_K$ is expected to be nonnegative after a suitable choice of foliation and current normalization on the nonsuperradiant sector. The remaining task is superradiant compensation. A precise abstract version is provided by Proposition~\ref{prop:ergosphere-compensation}: once the frequency-localized inequalities \eqref{eq:erg-comp-1}--\eqref{eq:erg-comp-3} are proved for the Kerr currents, one obtains positivity of the mixed density by choosing $\beta$ large enough. The Killing--Yano term $\beta E_{KY}$ is therefore intended to provide the positive control on the superradiant piece:
\begin{equation}
\beta E_{KY} \geq \beta \cdot c_{KY} \cdot |h|^2
\end{equation}
where $c_{KY} > 0$ would come from a positive commutator / hidden-symmetry coercivity statement.

\textbf{Step 4: Near-horizon region $\mathcal{R}_3$.}
Near the horizon, the red-shift effect dominates. The term $\gamma E_{red}$ satisfies:
\begin{equation}
\gamma E_{red} \gtrsim \gamma(\chi)\kappa^{-1} |\nabla_V h|^2
\end{equation}
for a coefficient $\gamma(\chi)$ chosen so that the red-shift term controls derivatives transverse to the horizon. The quantitative near-extremal dependence of $\gamma(\chi)$ is part of the target estimate.

\textbf{Step 5: Combining regions.}
At this point one can invoke Proposition~\ref{prop:regional-coercivity} with $q[h]\sim |\nabla h|^2$, provided the regional pointwise hypotheses are verified for the actual Kerr-adapted currents under consideration. The minimum constant $c_1(\chi)$ is then controlled by the worst of the exterior, ergoregion, and near-horizon constants.

The upper bound follows from the triangle inequality and $|E_{KY}| \leq C |\nabla h|^2$, with $C$ growing like $(1-\chi^2)^{-1/2}$ near extremality due to the expanding ergosphere volume.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The Nonlinear Energy Estimate}

\begin{theorem}[Conditional Nonlinear Energy Decay]\label{thm:nonlinear-energy-decay}
Let $h$ solve the nonlinear Einstein vacuum equations $G_{\mu\nu}[g_{Kerr} + h] = 0$ in a global gauge (e.g. generalized harmonic with a suitable constraint damping/modulation scheme) with initial data satisfying $\|h(0)\|_{H^s} \leq \epsilon$ for $s$ sufficiently large. Assume:
\begin{enumerate}
    \item coercivity in Assumption~\ref{thm:full-coercivity};
    \item a linear decay estimate for the linearized evolution compatible with $\mathcal{E}$ (e.g. via HHV-type resonance expansions and integrated decay);
    \item superradiant/trapping control in a form stable under the commutations required in the nonlinear iteration.
\end{enumerate}
Then, while a bootstrap smallness condition holds (e.g. $\|h(t)\|_{H^{s'}} \leq 2\epsilon$ for some $s' < s$), one obtains an inequality of the form:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:nonlinear-decay}
\frac{d}{dt}\mathcal{E}[h] \leq -2\gamma_0(\chi) \mathcal{E}[h] + C_{NL}(\chi) \mathcal{E}[h]^{3/2}
\end{equation}
for some $\gamma_0(\chi) > 0$ and $C_{NL}(\chi) > 0$ depending on $\chi=a/M$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{remark}[Superradiance and Derivative Loss]\label{rmk:nonlinear-issues}
This result relies on two key conditions: (1) The energy functional must control superradiant modes which can transiently grow before decaying; we assume here that the modified energy density effectively subtracts these unstable contributions. (2) The quasi-linear nature of the equations induces a loss of derivatives, requiring the use of a Nash-Moser iteration scheme or null structure exploitation to close the bootstrap at high regularity $H^s$.
\end{remark}

\begin{proof}[Proof strategy (conditional on the assumptions)]
The Einstein vacuum equations in a fixed global gauge, expanded around Kerr, take schematically the form
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{L}_{Kerr} h = N[h, h] + O(h^3)
\end{equation}
where $\mathcal{L}_{Kerr}$ is the linearized Einstein operator and $N[h, h]$ is the quadratic nonlinearity.

\textbf{Step 1: Linear energy decay.}
Assuming a HHV-type linear decay estimate compatible with $\mathcal{E}$, solutions to $\mathcal{L}_{Kerr} h_{lin} = 0$ satisfy:
\begin{equation}
\frac{d}{dt}\mathcal{E}[h_{lin}] \leq -2\gamma_0(\chi) \mathcal{E}[h_{lin}]
\end{equation}
This follows from a resonance expansion/spectral gap together with uniform energy and integrated decay estimates.

\textbf{Step 2: Null structure of nonlinearity.}
The Einstein equations possess the \textit{weak null condition}. In a suitable gauge (generalized harmonic), the nonlinearity takes the form:
\begin{equation}
N[h, h] = Q_0(\partial h, \partial h) + Q_1(h, \partial^2 h)
\end{equation}
where $Q_0$ contains only ``null forms'' satisfying:
\begin{equation}
|Q_0(\partial h, \partial h)| \lesssim |\partial_u h||\partial_v h| + |\slashed{\nabla} h|^2
\end{equation}
with $u, v$ being null coordinates. The term $Q_1$ is lower order.

\textbf{Step 3: Nonlinear contribution to energy.}
The nonlinear terms contribute to the energy derivative via integration by parts:
\begin{align}
\left|\frac{d}{dt}\mathcal{E}[h]\right|_{nonlinear} &= \left|\int_{\Sigma_t} \langle \nabla_t h, N[h,h] \rangle_g \, d\mu \right| \\
&\leq \|\nabla_t h\|_{L^2} \|N[h,h]\|_{L^2} \\
&\lesssim \mathcal{E}[h]^{1/2} \cdot \|\nabla h\|_{L^4}^2
\end{align}
where we used Hölder's inequality and the null form estimate $\|N[h,h]\|_{L^2} \lesssim \|\nabla h\|_{L^4}^2$.

\textbf{Step 4: Sobolev inequality.}
By Sobolev embedding on $\Sigma_t$ (which is 3-dimensional), $H^2(\Sigma_t) \hookrightarrow L^6(\Sigma_t)$ and by interpolation:
\begin{equation}
\|\nabla h\|_{L^4}^2 \lesssim \|\nabla h\|_{L^2} \|\nabla h\|_{L^6} \lesssim \|h\|_{H^1} \cdot \|h\|_{H^2} \lesssim c_1(\chi)^{-1} \mathcal{E}[h]
\end{equation}
where we used the coercivity bound $\|h\|_{H^1}^2 \leq c_1(\chi)^{-1} \mathcal{E}[h]$.

\textbf{Step 5: Combining.}
Combining Steps 3 and 4:
\begin{align}
\left|\frac{d}{dt}\mathcal{E}[h]\right|_{nonlinear} &\lesssim \mathcal{E}[h]^{1/2} \cdot c_1(\chi)^{-1} \mathcal{E}[h] = c_1(\chi)^{-1} \mathcal{E}[h]^{3/2}
\end{align}
Thus:
\begin{equation}
\frac{d}{dt}\mathcal{E}[h] \leq -2\gamma_0(\chi)\mathcal{E}[h] + C_{NL}(\chi) \mathcal{E}[h]^{3/2}
\end{equation}
where $C_{NL}(\chi) = C/c_1(\chi) \lesssim (1-\chi^2)^{-1/2}(1+\chi)^2$ by the coercivity bounds.
\end{proof}

\begin{corollary}[Global Decay for Small Data (Conditional)]\label{cor:global-decay}
If
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{E}[h](0) \le \left(\frac{\gamma_0(\chi)}{C_{NL}(\chi)}\right)^2,
\end{equation}
then the solution satisfies:
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{E}[h](t) \leq \mathcal{E}[h](0) \cdot e^{-\gamma_0(\chi) t}
\end{equation}
for all $t \geq 0$.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
Apply Proposition~\ref{thm:quantitative-bootstrap} with $E(t)=\mathcal{E}[h](t)$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{The Bootstrap Argument for Full Subextremal Kerr}\label{subsec:bootstrap-hierarchy}

\begin{theorem}[Nonlinear Stability of Kerr: Full Subextremal Range (Conditional)]\label{thm:main-nonlinear}
Assume:
\begin{enumerate}
    \item the coercive energy estimate in Assumption~\ref{thm:full-coercivity};
    \item a linear decay statement compatible with the commuted nonlinear system, for example of the type used in Theorem~\ref{thm:nonlinear-energy-decay};
    \item superradiant and trapping control stable under commutation;
    \item a local well-posedness and continuation criterion in the chosen global gauge/modulation framework.
\end{enumerate}
Let $(M_0, a_0)$ with $|a_0| < M_0$ and set $\chi_0 = |a_0|/M_0$. Then there exists $\epsilon_0 = \epsilon_0(\chi_0) > 0$ such that: if initial data $(\bar{g}, \bar{k})$ satisfies
\begin{equation}
\|(\bar{g} - g_{Kerr}, \bar{k} - k_{Kerr})\|_{H^8_{-1/2-\delta}(\Sigma_0)} \leq \epsilon < \epsilon_0,
\end{equation}
then the maximal development $(\mathcal{M}, g)$ is future geodesically complete and satisfies:
\begin{equation}
\|g(t) - g_{Kerr}(M_f, a_f)\|_{H^k(\Sigma_t)} \leq C_k(\chi_0) \cdot \epsilon \cdot (1 + t/M_f)^{-p_k}
\end{equation}
where $p_0 = 1$, $p_1 = 3/2$, $p_2 = 2$, and $(M_f, a_f)$ are the final Kerr parameters with $|a_f| < M_f$.
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
The proof uses a multi-level bootstrap with four norms tracking different aspects of the solution.

\textbf{Step 1: Norm hierarchy.}
Define the bootstrap norms:
\begin{align}
\mathcal{N}_0(T) &= \sup_{t \in [0,T]} (1+t)^{p_0} \|h(t)\|_{H^2(\Sigma_t)} \\
\mathcal{N}_1(T) &= \sup_{t \in [0,T]} (1+t)^{p_1} \|\partial h(t)\|_{H^2(\Sigma_t)} \\
\mathcal{N}_2(T) &= \sup_{t \in [0,T]} (1+t)^{p_2} \|\partial^2 h(t)\|_{H^2(\Sigma_t)} \\
\mathcal{N}_3(T) &= \left(\int_0^T \|h(t)\|_{H^3}^2 (1+t)^{2p_1+1} dt\right)^{1/2}
\end{align}

\textbf{Step 2: Bootstrap hypothesis.}
Assume for $t \in [0, T]$:
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{N}_i(T) \leq A_i \epsilon, \quad i = 0, 1, 2, 3
\end{equation}
with constants $A_i = 2C_i$ where $C_i$ come from the linear theory.

\textbf{Step 3: Linear estimates.}
Assuming coercivity (Assumption~\ref{thm:full-coercivity}) and a linear decay estimate compatible with the commutations used in the nonlinear iteration (e.g. a HHV-type resonance expansion plus integrated decay):
\begin{equation}
\|h_{lin}(t)\|_{H^k} \leq C_k e^{-\gamma_0 t} \|h_{lin}(0)\|_{H^{k+2}} + C_k' (1+t)^{-p_k} \|h_{lin}(0)\|_{H^{k+4}}
\end{equation}
The exponential decay comes from the spectral gap; the polynomial tail from the Price law.

\textbf{Step 4: Nonlinear estimates.}
The nonlinear term satisfies (using Theorem \ref{thm:nonlinear-energy-decay}):
\begin{equation}
\|N[h, h](t)\|_{H^{k-2}} \lesssim \|h(t)\|_{H^k}^2 \lesssim \frac{(A_0 \epsilon)^2}{(1+t)^{2p_0}}
\end{equation}

\textbf{Step 5: Duhamel formula.}
Writing $h = h_{lin} + h_{NL}$ where $h_{NL}$ solves $\mathcal{L}_{Kerr} h_{NL} = N[h, h]$:
\begin{equation}
h_{NL}(t) = \int_0^t e^{(t-s)\mathcal{L}_{Kerr}} N[h, h](s) \, ds
\end{equation}
Using the linear decay and nonlinear bounds with $p_0 = 1$:
\begin{align}
\|h_{NL}(t)\|_{H^k} &\lesssim \int_0^t (1+(t-s))^{-p_k} \|N[h,h](s)\|_{H^{k-2}} \, ds \\
&\lesssim (A_0 \epsilon)^2 \int_0^t \frac{(1+(t-s))^{-p_k}}{(1+s)^{2}} \, ds
\end{align}
For $p_k \leq 2$, splitting at $s = t/2$:
\begin{align}
\int_0^t \frac{(1+(t-s))^{-p_k}}{(1+s)^{2}} ds &\leq \int_0^{t/2} \frac{(1+t/2)^{-p_k}}{(1+s)^2} ds + \int_{t/2}^t \frac{1}{(1+t/2)^2} ds \\
&\lesssim (1+t)^{-p_k} + (1+t)^{-1} \lesssim (1+t)^{-p_k}
\end{align}
Thus $\|h_{NL}(t)\|_{H^k} \lesssim (A_0\epsilon)^2 (1+t)^{-p_k}$.

\textbf{Step 6: Bootstrap improvement.}
From Steps 3-5, the total norm satisfies:
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{N}_i(T) \leq C_i \epsilon + K_i (A_0 \epsilon)^2 = C_i \epsilon \left(1 + \frac{K_i A_0^2}{C_i} \epsilon\right)
\end{equation}
Since $A_i = 2C_i$, for $\epsilon < C_i/(4K_i C_i^2) = 1/(4K_i C_i)$:
\begin{equation}
\mathcal{N}_i(T) \leq C_i \epsilon \cdot \frac{3}{2} < 2C_i \epsilon = A_i \epsilon
\end{equation}
This strictly improves the bootstrap hypothesis.

\textbf{Step 7: Continuation.}
By continuity, the bootstrap holds for all $T < \infty$. Global existence follows.

\textbf{Step 8: Final state identification.}
The Bondi mass and angular momentum:
\begin{align}
M_f &= M_0 - \int_0^\infty \frac{dM_{Bondi}}{dt} dt = M_0 - O(\epsilon^2) \\
J_f &= J_0 - \int_0^\infty \frac{dJ_{Bondi}}{dt} dt = J_0 - O(\epsilon^2)
\end{align}
are well-defined by the integrability of the news function, guaranteed by the decay estimates.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Near-Extremal Refinement}

The most delicate case is $\chi_0 \to 1$. We establish uniform estimates.

\begin{conjecture}[Uniform Near-Extremal Stability]\label{thm:near-extremal-uniform}
For $\chi_0 = 1 - \epsilon_{ext}$ with $\epsilon_{ext} \in (0, 1)$, one expects the constants in Theorem \ref{thm:main-nonlinear} to satisfy polynomial bounds of the form
\begin{align}
\epsilon_0(\chi_0) &\geq c_0 \cdot \epsilon_{ext}^{2} \\
C_k(\chi_0) &\leq C_k^{(0)} \cdot \epsilon_{ext}^{-1/2}
\end{align}
after suitable choices of norms and bootstrap quantities. In particular, one expects stability to persist for every fixed $\chi_0<1$, while the basin of attraction shrinks as $\chi_0 \to 1$.
\end{conjecture}

\begin{proof}[Heuristic bookkeeping]
The exponents displayed above are not proved; they arise from a simple consistency check on how the constants might degrade near extremality. The degradation comes from three sources:

\textbf{1. Spectral gap:} one expects a lower bound of the form $\gamma_0(\chi_0) \gtrsim \sqrt{\epsilon_{ext}}/M$ if the coercivity-to-gap mechanism degenerates at the natural near-extremal rate.

\textbf{2. Coercivity constant:} one expects $c_1(\chi_0)$ to deteriorate polynomially, for instance like $\epsilon_{ext}^{1/2}$, in accordance with the target coercivity assumption.

\textbf{3. Nonlinear constant:} $C_{NL}(\chi_0) \sim \epsilon_{ext}^{-1/2}$ (corrected from the refined Sobolev analysis).

The critical balance from Corollary \ref{cor:global-decay} is:
\begin{equation}
\epsilon_0 = \frac{\gamma_0^2}{4 C_{NL}^2} \sim \frac{\epsilon_{ext}}{4 \cdot \epsilon_{ext}^{-1}} = \frac{\epsilon_{ext}^2}{4}
\end{equation}
which motivates the conjectural bound $\epsilon_0 \gtrsim \epsilon_{ext}^2$.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Structure of Nonlinear Terms: Null Condition}

A key technical ingredient is the structure of the Einstein equations' nonlinearity.

\begin{assumption}[Weak Null Structure for Kerr Perturbations]\label{thm:null-condition}
In a generalized harmonic gauge adapted to Kerr, one expects the vacuum Einstein equations $G_{\mu\nu}[g] = 0$ to take the reduced form
\begin{equation}
\Box_{g_{Kerr}} h_{\mu\nu} = N_{\mu\nu}[h, h] + R_{\mu\nu}[h, h, h]
\end{equation}
where the quadratic nonlinearity exhibits a weak-null-type structure:
\begin{equation}
N_{\mu\nu}[h, h] = Q_{\mu\nu}(\partial h, \partial h) + L_{\mu\nu}(h, \partial^2 h)
\end{equation}
with $Q_{\mu\nu}$ obeying an estimate of the schematic form
\begin{equation}\label{eq:null-form-bound}
|Q_{\mu\nu}(\partial h, \partial h)| \lesssim |\partial_u h||\partial_v h| + |\slashed{\nabla} h|^2
\end{equation}
and $L_{\mu\nu}$ lower order from the point of view of the nonlinear iteration.
\end{assumption}

\begin{proof}[Heuristic origin]
In generalized harmonic gauge with source $H^\mu = g_{Kerr}^{\alpha\beta}\Gamma^\mu_{\alpha\beta}[g_{Kerr}]$, the reduced Einstein equations take the schematic form
\begin{equation}
g^{\alpha\beta} \partial_\alpha \partial_\beta g_{\mu\nu} = F_{\mu\nu}(g, \partial g)
\end{equation}
where $F_{\mu\nu}$ contains products of Christoffel symbols and their derivatives.

The key observation is that the principal part of $F_{\mu\nu}$ has the structure:
\begin{equation}
F_{\mu\nu} = g^{\alpha\beta}g^{\gamma\delta}(\partial_\alpha g_{\mu\gamma}\partial_\beta g_{\nu\delta} - \partial_\alpha g_{\mu\nu} \partial_\beta g_{\gamma\delta}) + \text{l.o.t.}
\end{equation}
Using the double null decomposition $g^{\alpha\beta} = -\frac{1}{2}(l^\alpha \underline{l}^\beta + \underline{l}^\alpha l^\beta) + \slashed{g}^{\alpha\beta}$:
\begin{align}
g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_\alpha h \cdot \partial_\beta h &= -l^\alpha \underline{l}^\beta \partial_\alpha h \cdot \partial_\beta h + \slashed{g}^{\alpha\beta}\partial_\alpha h \cdot \partial_\beta h \\
&= -\partial_u h \cdot \partial_v h + |\slashed{\nabla} h|^2
\end{align}
This indicates why one expects a null-form estimate such as \eqref{eq:null-form-bound}, but a full derivation in a Kerr-adapted nonlinear gauge is not carried out here.
\end{proof}

\begin{remark}[What this hypothesis would buy]
If the weak null structure assumption is available together with the required bilinear estimates, one expects
\begin{equation}
\int_0^\infty \|N[h, h](t)\|_{L^2} \, dt < \infty
\end{equation}
for solutions with $\mathcal{N}_1(T) < \infty$, which is the form needed to close the bootstrap.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Conditional Proof Summary}

We summarize the logical structure of the conditional scheme:

    node distance=1.2cm,
